### Socket.IO Client Communication in JavaScript

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3

Illustrates how to establish a Socket.IO connection and handle messages and custom events on the client-side using the Socket.IO JavaScript library. It shows sending messages with 'send' and 'emit', and receiving with 'on'.

```javascript
const socket = io("ws://localhost:3000");

socket.on("connect", () => {
  // either with send()
  socket.send("Hello!");

  // or with emit() and custom event names
  socket.emit("salutations", "Hello!", { "mr": "john" }, Uint8Array.from([1, 2, 3, 4]));
});

// handle the event sent with socket.send()
socket.on("message", data => {
  console.log(data);
});

// handle the event sent with socket.emit()
socket.on("greetings", (elem1, elem2, elem3) => {
  console.log(elem1, elem2, elem3);
});

```

--------------------------------

### Manager Options

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/client-initialization

Configure settings that are shared across all Socket instances managed by the same Socket.IO Manager.

```APIDOC
## Manager Options

*   **Description**: These settings will be shared by all Socket instances attached to the same Manager.

### `reconnection`

*   **Description**: Whether reconnection is enabled or not. If set to `false`, you need to manually reconnect.
*   **Default Value**: `true`
*   **Type**: `boolean`

*   **Manual Reconnection Example**:
    ```javascript
    import { io } from "socket.io-client";

    const socket = io({
      reconnection: false
    });

    const tryReconnect = () => {
      setTimeout(() => {
        socket.io.open((err) => {
          if (err) {
            tryReconnect();
          }
        });
      }, 2000);
    }

    socket.io.on("close", tryReconnect);
    ```

### `reconnectionAttempts`

*   **Description**: The number of reconnection attempts before giving up.
*   **Default Value**: `Infinity`
*   **Type**: `number`

### `reconnectionDelay`

*   **Description**: The initial delay before reconnection in milliseconds (affected by the `randomizationFactor` value).
*   **Default Value**: `1000`
*   **Type**: `number`

### `reconnectionDelayMax`

*   **Description**: The maximum delay between two reconnection attempts. Each attempt increases the reconnection delay by 2x.
*   **Default Value**: `5000`
*   **Type**: `number`

### `randomizationFactor`

*   **Description**: The randomization factor used when reconnecting (so that clients do not reconnect at the exact same time after a server crash, for example).
*   **Default Value**: `0.5`
*   **Type**: `number`

*   **Example with default values**: 
    *   1st reconnection attempt: between 500 and 1500 ms (`1000 * 2^0 * (<something between -0.5 and 1.5>)`)
    *   2nd reconnection attempt: between 1000 and 3000 ms (`1000 * 2^1 * (<something between -0.5 and 1.5>)`)
    *   3rd reconnection attempt: between 2000 and 5000 ms (`1000 * 2^2 * (<something between -0.5 and 1.5>)`)
    *   Subsequent attempts: after 5000 ms

### `timeout`

*   **Description**: The timeout in milliseconds for each connection attempt.
*   **Default Value**: `20000`
*   **Type**: `number`

### `autoConnect`

*   **Description**: Whether to automatically connect upon creation. If set to `false`, you need to manually connect.
*   **Default Value**: `true`
*   **Type**: `boolean`

*   **Manual Connection Example**:
    ```javascript
    import { io } from "socket.io-client";

    const socket = io({
      autoConnect: false
    });

    socket.connect();
    // or
    socket.io.open();
    ```

### `parser`

*   **Description**: The parser used to marshall/unmarshall packets. Please see [here](link-to-parser-docs) for more information.
*   **Added in**: v2.2.0
*   **Default Value**: `require("socket.io-parser")`
*   **Type**: `object`
```

### Socket.IO Socket.binary() Removal and Custom Parser Implementation

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

The Socket.binary() method in Socket.IO, used to indicate the absence of binary data, has been removed. Its functionality is replaced by the ability to provide a custom parser during Socket.IO server initialization, offering more flexibility.

```javascript
socket.binary(false).emit("hello", "no binary");

```

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {
  parser: myCustomParser
});

```

### Handle Socket Disconnecting Event (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/server-socket-instance

This example shows how to use the 'disconnecting' event in Socket.IO. This event fires just before a socket disconnects, allowing you to perform actions like notifying other users in a room that a user is leaving.

```javascript
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("disconnecting", (reason) => {
    for (const room of socket.rooms) {
      if (room !== socket.id) {
        socket.to(room).emit("user has left", socket.id);
      }
    }
  });
});
```

### Initialize Socket.IO Client using Manager

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/client-api

This snippet provides an alternative method for initializing a Socket.IO client connection by first creating a `Manager` instance and then obtaining a socket for a specific namespace. This approach allows for more centralized configuration of connection options.

```javascript
const { Manager } = require("socket.io-client");  
  
const manager = new Manager("ws://example.com", {  
  reconnectionDelayMax: 10000,  
  query: {  
    "my-key": "my-value"  
  }  
});  
  
const socket = manager.socket("/my-namespace", {  
  auth: {  
    token: "123"  
  }  
});  
```

### Set Up SQLite Database and Socket.IO Server (ES Modules)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/tutorial/step-7

Initializes an Express server, sets up a SQLite database to store chat messages with unique offsets, and configures Socket.IO for real-time communication. This code uses ES module syntax.

```javascript
import express from 'express';  
import { createServer } from 'node:http';  
import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url';  
import { dirname, join } from 'node:path';  
import { Server } from 'socket.io';  
import sqlite3 from 'sqlite3';  
import { open } from 'sqlite';  
  
// open the database file  
const db = await open({  
  filename: 'chat.db',  
  driver: sqlite3.Database  
});  
  
// create our 'messages' table (you can ignore the 'client_offset' column for now)  
await db.exec(`  
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (  
      id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,  
      client_offset TEXT UNIQUE,  
      content TEXT  
  );  
`);  
  
const app = express();  
const server = createServer(app);  
const io = new Server(server, {  
  connectionStateRecovery: {}  
});  
  
const __dirname = dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url));  
  
app.get('/', (req, res) => {  
  res.sendFile(join(__dirname, 'index.html'));  
});  
  
io.on('connection', (socket) => {  
  socket.on('chat message', async (msg) => {  
    let result;  
    try {  
      // store the message in the database  
      result = await db.run('INSERT INTO messages (content) VALUES (?)', msg);  
    } catch (e) {  
      // TODO handle the failure  
      return;  
    }  
    // include the offset with the message  
    io.emit('chat message', msg, result.lastID);  
  });  
});  
  
server.listen(3000, () => {  
  console.log('server running at http://localhost:3000');  
});  

```

### Bind Socket.IO Server to an Engine.IO Server

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/server-api

Binds the Socket.IO server to a pre-existing Engine.IO server instance. This is an advanced option for custom setups where you need to control the underlying Engine.IO server directly. Requires 'engine.io' and 'socket.io' packages.

```javascript
import { createServer } from "node:http";
import { Server as Engine } from "engine.io";
import { Server } from "socket.io";

const httpServer = createServer((req, res) => {
  res.writeHead(404).end();
});

const engine = new Engine();

engine.attach(httpServer, {
  path: "/socket.io/"
});

const io = new Server();

io.bind(engine);

httpServer.listen(3000);

```

Within a Socket.IO connection handler, this snippet shows how to retrieve the client's protocol version. This is useful for monitoring upgrades and ensuring compatibility, as the version is also reflected in the EIO query parameter.

```javascript
io.on("connection", (socket) => {  
  const version = socket.conn.protocol; // either 3 or 4  
});  
```

### Socket.IO Unauthorized Event Middleware

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/server-socket-instance

This example demonstrates how to use Socket.IO server-side middleware for authorization. If an event is deemed unauthorized, an 'unauthorized event' error is returned to 'next()'. The server then listens for the 'error' event to disconnect unauthorized clients.

```javascript
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.use(([event, ...args], next) => {
    if (isUnauthorized(event)) {
      return next(new Error("unauthorized event"));
    }
    next();
  });

  socket.on("error", (err) => {
    if (err && err.message === "unauthorized event") {
      socket.disconnect();
    }
  });
});
```

### HTTP Polling Request (HTTP)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v2/internals

Represents the initial HTTP GET request made by the client to establish a polling transport with the Socket.IO server. It includes protocol version, transport type, and cache-busting parameters.

```http
GET https://myhost.com/socket.io/?EIO=3&transport=polling&t=ML4jUwU&b64=1  
  
with:  
  
  "EIO=3"               # the current version of the Engine.IO protocol  
  "transport=polling"   # the transport being established  
  "t=ML4jUwU&b64=1"     # a hashed timestamp for cache-busting  
  
```

### Configure Socket.IO with Redis Adapter

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/server-initialization

This snippet demonstrates how to configure Socket.IO to use the Redis adapter for inter-server communication. It requires the 'redis' package and 'socket.io-redis' package. The Redis client is created and then passed to the adapter configuration.

```javascript
const httpServer = require("http").createServer();
const redisClient = require("redis").createClient();
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {
  adapter: require("socket.io-redis")({
    pubClient: redisClient,
    subClient: redisClient.duplicate()
  })
});

```

### Conditional Passport JWT Authentication for Socket.IO Handshake

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/middlewares

Provides an example of using `passport-jwt` with Socket.IO's engine middleware. It conditionally applies JWT authentication only to the initial handshake request, identified by the absence of the `sid` query parameter.

```javascript
io.engine.use((req, res, next) => {
  const isHandshake = req._query.sid === undefined;
  if (isHandshake) {
    passport.authenticate("jwt", { session: false })(req, res, next);
  } else {
    next();
  }
});
```

### Node.js Cluster Sticky Session with @socket.io/sticky

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/using-multiple-nodes

Implements sticky session load balancing for Socket.IO using Node.js's built-in cluster module and the '@socket.io/sticky' package. The master process sets up the HTTP server and load balancing method, while worker processes handle Socket.IO connections. This example demonstrates basic clustering and worker management.

```javascript
const cluster = require("cluster");
const http = require("http");
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const redisAdapter = require("socket.io-redis");
const numCPUs = require("os").cpus().length;
const { setupMaster, setupWorker } = require("@socket.io/sticky");

if (cluster.isMaster) {
  console.log(`Master ${process.pid} is running`);

  const httpServer = http.createServer();
  setupMaster(httpServer, {
    loadBalancingMethod: "least-connection", // either "random", "round-robin" or "least-connection"
  });
  httpServer.listen(3000);

  for (let i = 0; i < numCPUs; i++) {
    cluster.fork();
  }

  cluster.on("exit", (worker) => {
    console.log(`Worker ${worker.process.pid} died`);
    cluster.fork();
  });
} else {
  console.log(`Worker ${process.pid} started`);

  const httpServer = http.createServer();
  const io = new Server(httpServer);
  io.adapter(redisAdapter({ host: "localhost", port: 6379 }));
  setupWorker(io);

  io.on("connection", (socket) => {
    /* ... */
  });
}

```

### Socket Handshake Details Object

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/server-socket-instance

Provides the structure and an example of the handshake object available on the Socket instance. This object contains information about the initial connection, including headers, query parameters, authentication payload, timestamps, URL, client address, and security details.

```json
{
  "headers": {
    "user-agent": "xxxx",
    "accept": "*/*",
    "host": "example.com",
    "connection": "close"
  },
  "query": {
    "EIO": "4",
    "transport": "polling",
    "t": "NNjNltH"
  },
  "auth": {
    "token": "123"
  },
  "time": "Sun Nov 22 2020 01:33:46 GMT+0100 (Central European Standard Time)",
  "issued": 1606005226969,
  "url": "/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling&t=NNjNltH",
  "address": "::ffff:1.2.3.4",
  "xdomain": false,
  "secure": true
}
```

### Advanced Client Connection with Options

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/client-api

Illustrates how to establish a Socket.IO client connection with custom options, including reconnection settings, authentication tokens, and query parameters.

```APIDOC
## Advanced Client Connection with Options

### Description
Establishes a new Socket.IO client connection with advanced options, allowing customization of reconnection behavior, authentication, and query parameters. This method provides more control over the connection process compared to the basic `io()` call.

### Method
`io(url, options)`

### Endpoint
N/A (Client-side function)

### Parameters
#### Path Parameters
None

#### Query Parameters
None

#### Request Body
None

### Parameters
* `url` (String) - The URL of the Socket.IO server. Defaults to `window.location.host`.
* `options` (Object) - Configuration options for the connection.
  * `forceNew` (Boolean) - If `true`, forces a new connection, preventing reuse of an existing manager.
  * `reconnectionDelayMax` (Number) - The maximum delay in milliseconds between reconnection attempts.
  * `auth` (Object) - Authentication credentials to be sent with the connection request.
    * `token` (String) - The authentication token.
  * `query` (Object) - Query parameters to be appended to the connection URL.
    * `"my-key"` (String) - Example query parameter.

### Request Example
```javascript
const io = require("socket.io-client");

const socket = io("ws://example.com/my-namespace", {
  reconnectionDelayMax: 10000,
  auth: {
    token: "123"
  },
  query: {
    "my-key": "my-value"
  }
});
```

### Shorter Version using Manager
```javascript
const { Manager } = require("socket.io-client");

const manager = new Manager("ws://example.com", {
  reconnectionDelayMax: 10000,
  query: {
    "my-key": "my-value"
  }
});

const socket = manager.socket("/my-namespace", {
  auth: {
    token: "123"
  }
});
```

### Response
#### Success Response (200)
Returns a `Socket` instance configured with the provided options.

#### Response Example
```json
{
  "type": "Socket",
  "description": "Represents a connected Socket.IO client with custom options."
}
```
```

### Rollup.js Client Configuration for Node.js

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/client-with-bundlers

Configures Rollup.js for bundling the socket.io client in a Node.js environment. This setup includes plugins for resolving modules and handling CommonJS, along with uglify for minification. The `preferBuiltins: true` option in `resolve` is crucial for Node.js specific module resolution.

```bash
npm i -D socket.io-client rollup @rollup/plugin-node-resolve @rollup/plugin-commonjs rollup-plugin-uglify  
```

```javascript
import resolve from "@rollup/plugin-node-resolve";
import commonjs from "@rollup/plugin-commonjs";
import { uglify } from "rollup-plugin-uglify";

export default {
  input: "index.js",
  output: {
    file: "bundle.js",
    format: "cjs",
  },
  plugins: [
    resolve({
      preferBuiltins: true,
    }),
    commonjs(),
    uglify(),
  ],
};

```

### TypeScript Typed Events - Client-Side

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-3-x-to-4-0

Socket.IO v4 enhances TypeScript support by allowing explicit typing of events and their arguments on the client-side. This improves code safety and provides better IDE autocompletion.

```typescript
interface ClientToServerEvents {
  noArg: () => void;
  basicEmit: (a: number, b: string, c: number[]) => void;
}

interface ServerToClientEvents {
  withAck: (d: string, cb: (e: number) => void) => void;
}

import { io, Socket } from "socket.io-client";

const socket: Socket<ServerToClientEvents, ClientToServerEvents> = io();

socket.emit("noArg");

socket.emit("basicEmit", 1, "2", [3]);

socket.on("withAck", (d, cb) => {
    cb(4);
});
```

--------------------------------

### Socket.IO Cluster Setup with ES Modules

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/tutorial/step-9

Implements a horizontally scalable Socket.IO server using ES modules, Node.js's `cluster` module, and the `@socket.io/cluster-adapter`. This version configures the primary process to manage worker threads and utilizes the adapter for efficient communication across server instances.

```javascript
import express from 'express';  
import { createServer } from 'node:http';  
import { Server } from 'socket.io';  
import sqlite3 from 'sqlite3';  
import { open } from 'sqlite';  
import { availableParallelism } from 'node:os';  
import cluster from 'node:cluster';  
import { createAdapter, setupPrimary } from '@socket.io/cluster-adapter';  
  
if (cluster.isPrimary) {  
  const numCPUs = availableParallelism();  
  // create one worker per available core  
  for (let i = 0; i < numCPUs; i++) {  
    cluster.fork({  
      PORT: 3000 + i  
    });  
  }  
    
  // set up the adapter on the primary thread  
  setupPrimary();  
} else {  
  const app = express();  
  const server = createServer(app);  
  const io = new Server(server, {  
    connectionStateRecovery: {},  
    // set up the adapter on each worker thread  
    adapter: createAdapter()  
  });  
  
  // [...]  
  
  // each worker will listen on a distinct port  
  const port = process.env.PORT;  
  
  server.listen(port, () => {  
    console.log(`server running at http://localhost:${port}`);  
  });  
}  


```

--------------------------------

### Socket.IO Packet Encoding Differences (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v2/migrating-from-0-9

Highlights the change in Socket.IO's packet encoding mechanism. Encoding is now class-based and asynchronous, using a callback that provides an array of encodings, which is more flexible for handling binary data.

```javascript
var encoding = parser.encode(packet);
console.log(encoding); // fully encoded packet
```

```javascript
var encoder = new parser.Encoder();
encoder.encode(packet, function(encodings) {
  for (var i = 0; i < encodings.length; i++) {
    console.log(encodings[i]); // encoded parts of the packet
  }
});
```

--------------------------------

### Distinguishing Manager and Socket Query Options in Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Illustrates the evolution of handling query parameters for HTTP requests and CONNECT packets. Previously, the 'query' option served both purposes. Now, 'query' is for the Manager (HTTP parameters) and 'auth' is for the Socket (CONNECT packet), providing a clearer distinction and preventing multiplexing issues.

```javascript
const socket = io({
  query: {
    token: "abc"
  }
});
```

```javascript
const { Manager } = require("socket.io-client");

// a new Manager is created (which will manage the low-level connection)
const manager = new Manager({
  query: { // sent in the query parameters
    token: "abc"
  }
});

// and then a Socket instance is created for the namespace (here, the main namespace, "/")
const socket = manager.socket("/", {
  query: { // sent in the CONNECT packet
    token: "abc"
  }
});
```

```javascript
// client-side
const socket1 = io({
  query: {
    token: "abc"
  }
});

const socket2 = io("/my-namespace", {
  query: {
    token: "def"
  }
});

// server-side
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log(socket.handshake.query.token); // abc (ok!)
});

io.of("/my-namespace").on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log(socket.handshake.query.token); // abc (what?)
});
```

```javascript
// plain object
const socket = io({
  auth: {
    token: "abc"
  }
});

// or with a function
const socket = io({
  auth: (cb) => {
    cb({
      token: "abc"
    });
  }
});

// server-side
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log(socket.handshake.auth.token); // abc
});
```

### Measuring Latency with Custom Ping/Pong

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Illustrates how to manually implement latency measurement after the removal of the 'pong' event. The server listens for a 'ping' event and optionally calls a callback, while the client uses `setInterval` to emit 'ping' events and calculate the round-trip time.

```javascript
// server-side  
io.on("connection", (socket) => {  
  socket.on("ping", (cb) => {  
    if (typeof cb === "function")  
      cb();  
  });  
});  

// client-side  
setInterval(() => {  
  const start = Date.now();  
  
  // volatile, so the packet will be discarded if the socket is not connected  
  socket.volatile.emit("ping", () => {  
    const latency = Date.now() - start;  
    // ...  
  });  
}, 5000);  
```

### Handling Non-String Room Names

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Illustrates the change in how room names are handled. Room names are no longer coerced to strings, allowing for distinct room names even if they have the same string representation (e.g., the number 42 and the string "42").

```javascript
// one way  
socket.join("42");  
io.to("42").emit("hello");  
// or another  
socket.join(42);  
io.to(42).emit("hello");  
```

### Configure Socket.IO Server with AWS SQS Adapter

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/aws-sqs-adapter

This JavaScript code demonstrates how to configure a Socket.IO server to use the AWS SQS adapter. It initializes AWS SNS and SQS clients, creates the adapter, and attaches it to the Socket.IO server instance. The adapter is then initialized before the server starts listening.

```javascript
import { SNS } from "@aws-sdk/client-sns";
import { SQS } from "@aws-sdk/client-sqs";
import { Server } from "socket.io";
import { createAdapter } from "@socket.io/aws-sqs-adapter";

const snsClient = new SNS();
const sqsClient = new SQS();

const io = new Server({
  adapter: createAdapter(snsClient, sqsClient)
});

// wait for the creation of the SQS queue
await io.of("/").adapter.init();

io.listen(3000);
```

### Client-Side Chat Message Handling (ES6)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/tutorial/step-5

This client-side JavaScript (ES6) code connects to the Socket.IO server, sends a 'chat message' on form submission, and listens for incoming 'chat message' events to display them. It requires the `socket.io.js` client library and assumes HTML elements with IDs 'form', 'input', and 'messages' exist.

```html
<script src="/socket.io/socket.io.js"></script>
<script>
  const socket = io();

  const form = document.getElementById('form');
  const input = document.getElementById('input');
  const messages = document.getElementById('messages');

  form.addEventListener('submit', (e) => {
    e.preventDefault();
    if (input.value) {
      socket.emit('chat message', input.value);
      input.value = '';
    }
  });

  socket.on('chat message', (msg) => {
    const item = document.createElement('li');
    item.textContent = msg;
    messages.appendChild(item);
    window.scrollTo(0, document.body.scrollHeight);
  });
</script>
```

--------------------------------

### Middleware Error Handling in Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Demonstrates the change in how middleware errors are handled and emitted. Previously, the 'error' event was used with a generic error object. Now, the 'connect_error' event is used, emitting a proper Error object with additional data.

```javascript
// server-side
io.use((socket, next) => {
  next(new Error("not authorized"));
});

// client-side
socket.on("error", err => {
  console.log(err); // not authorized
});

// or with an object
// server-side
io.use((socket, next) => {
  const err = new Error("not authorized");
  err.data = { content: "Please retry later" }; // additional details
  next(err);
});

// client-side
socket.on("error", err => {
  console.log(err); // { content: "Please retry later" }
});
```

```javascript
// server-side
io.use((socket, next) => {
  const err = new Error("not authorized");
  err.data = { content: "Please retry later" }; // additional details
  next(err);
});

// client-side
socket.on("connect_error", err => {
  console.log(err instanceof Error); // true
  console.log(err.message); // not authorized
  console.log(err.data); // { content: "Please retry later" }
});
```

### Socket.IO Broadcasting with Room Exclusion (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-3-x-to-4-0

Demonstrates the new `except` method in Socket.IO for excluding specific rooms during broadcasts. This allows for more granular control over message distribution, enabling scenarios where messages should be sent to all clients except those in a particular room.

```javascript
io.except("room1").emit(/* ... */); // to all clients except the ones in "room1"
io.to("room2").except("room3").emit(/* ... */); // to all clients in "room2" except the ones in "room3"

socket.broadcast.except("room1").emit(/* ... */); // to all clients except the ones in "room1" and the sender
socket.except("room1").emit(/* ... */); // same as above
socket.to("room4").except("room5").emit(/* ... */); // to all clients in "room4" except the ones in "room5" and the sender
```

### Socket.IO Socket Rooms - rooms property is now a Set

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

The Socket.rooms property in Socket.IO, which previously held an object representing the rooms a socket is in, is now an ES6 Set. This change affects how to access and check for rooms a socket belongs to.

```javascript
io.on("connection", (socket) => {

  console.log(Object.keys(socket.rooms)); // [ <socket.id> ]

  socket.join("room1");

  console.log(Object.keys(socket.rooms)); // [ <socket.id>, "room1" ]

});

```

```javascript
io.on("connection", (socket) => {

  console.log(socket.rooms); // Set { <socket.id> }

  socket.join("room1");

  console.log(socket.rooms); // Set { <socket.id>, "room1" }

});

```

### Customize Request Headers - Node.js

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/server-api

Listens for the 'headers' event on the engine.IO server to customize response headers for each HTTP request, including WebSocket upgrades. This example demonstrates adding or modifying 'set-cookie' headers based on incoming cookies.

```javascript
import { serialize, parse } from "cookie";  
  
io.engine.on("headers", (headers, request) => {  
  if (!request.headers.cookie) return;  
  const cookies = parse(request.headers.cookie);  
  if (!cookies.randomId) {  
    headers["set-cookie"] = serialize("randomId", "abc", { maxAge: 86400 });  
  }  
});  

```

### Access Socket ID and Manager Reference (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/client-api

Shows how to access the unique socket session identifier (`socket.id`) after a connection is established and how to get a reference to the underlying Manager object (`socket.io`) to interact with the engine's transport and packet events.

```javascript
const socket = io();

console.log(socket.id); // undefined

socket.on("connect", () => {
  console.log(socket.id); // "G5p5..."
});

```

```javascript
socket.on("connect", () => {
  const engine = socket.io.engine;
  console.log(engine.transport.name); // in most cases, prints "polling"

  engine.once("upgrade", () => {
    // called when the transport is upgraded (i.e. from HTTP long-polling to WebSocket)
    console.log(engine.transport.name); // in most cases, prints "websocket"
  });

  engine.on("packet", ({ type, data }) => {
    // called for each packet received
  });

  engine.on("packetCreate", ({ type, data }) => {
    // called for each packet sent
  });

  engine.on("drain", () => {
    // called when the write buffer is drained
  });

  engine.on("close", (reason) => {
    // called when the underlying connection is closed
  });
});

```

### Setup Clustered Socket.IO Server with SQLite (ES Modules)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/tutorial/ending-notes

This ES Module code snippet configures a clustered Node.js server using Socket.IO for real-time chat functionality. It establishes a connection to a SQLite database, ensures the 'messages' table exists, and manages client connections and messages. This version uses modern ES Module syntax and the `@socket.io/cluster-adapter` for multi-process scaling.

```javascript
import express from 'express';
import { createServer } from 'node:http';
import { fileURLToPath } from 'node:url';
import { dirname, join } from 'node:path';
import { Server } from 'socket.io';
import sqlite3 from 'sqlite3';
import { open } from 'sqlite';
import { availableParallelism } from 'node:os';
import cluster from 'node:cluster';
import { createAdapter, setupPrimary } from '@socket.io/cluster-adapter';

if (cluster.isPrimary) {
  const numCPUs = availableParallelism();
  for (let i = 0; i < numCPUs; i++) {
    cluster.fork({
      PORT: 3000 + i
    });
  }

  setupPrimary();
} else {
  const db = await open({
    filename: 'chat.db',
    driver: sqlite3.Database
  });

  await db.exec(`
    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (
      id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,
      client_offset TEXT UNIQUE,
      content TEXT
    );
  `);

  const app = express();
  const server = createServer(app);
  const io = new Server(server, {
    connectionStateRecovery: {},
    adapter: createAdapter()
  });

  const __dirname = dirname(fileURLToPath(import.meta.url));

  app.get('/', (req, res) => {
    res.sendFile(join(__dirname, 'index.html'));
  });

  io.on('connection', async (socket) => {
    socket.on('chat message', async (msg, clientOffset, callback) => {
      let result;
      try {
        result = await db.run('INSERT INTO messages (content, client_offset) VALUES (?, ?)', msg, clientOffset);
      } catch (e) {
        if (e.errno === 19 /* SQLITE_CONSTRAINT */ ) {
          callback();
        } else {
          // nothing to do, just let the client retry
        }
        return;
      }
      io.emit('chat message', msg, result.lastID);
      callback();
    });

    if (!socket.recovered) {
      try {
        await db.each('SELECT id, content FROM messages WHERE id > ?',
          [socket.handshake.auth.serverOffset || 0],
          (_err, row) => {
            socket.emit('chat message', row.content, row.id);
          }
        )
      } catch (e) {
        // something went wrong
      }
    }
  });

  const port = process.env.PORT;

  server.listen(port, () => {
    console.log(`server running at http://localhost:${port}`);
  });
}

```

### Socket.IO Test Suite with Mocha and Chai (TypeScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/testing

This TypeScript test suite leverages type safety for Socket.IO testing with Mocha and Chai. It includes type definitions for sockets and server addresses, enhancing code clarity and reducing potential runtime errors. The testing logic remains consistent with the JavaScript versions.

```typescript
import { createServer } from "node:http";  
import { type AddressInfo } from "node:net";  
import { io as ioc, type Socket as ClientSocket } from "socket.io-client";  
import { Server, type Socket as ServerSocket } from "socket.io";  
import { assert } from "chai";  
  
function waitFor(socket: ServerSocket | ClientSocket, event: string) {  
  return new Promise((resolve) => {  
    socket.once(event, resolve);  
  });  
}  
  
describe("my awesome project", () => {  
  let io: Server, serverSocket: ServerSocket, clientSocket: ClientSocket;  
  
  before((done) => {  
    const httpServer = createServer();  
    io = new Server(httpServer);  
    httpServer.listen(() => {  
      const port = (httpServer.address() as AddressInfo).port;  
      clientSocket = ioc(`http://localhost:${port}`);  
      io.on("connection", (socket) => {  
        serverSocket = socket;  
      });  
      clientSocket.on("connect", done);  
    });  
  });  
  
  after(() => {  
    io.close();  
    clientSocket.disconnect();  
  });  
  
  it("should work", (done) => {  
    clientSocket.on("hello", (arg) => {  
      assert.equal(arg, "world");  
      done();  
    });  
    serverSocket.emit("hello", "world");  
  });  
  

```

### Engine.IO Parser Encoded Message (Text)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v2/internals

An example of how the Engine.IO parser encodes the handshake data into a string format. It includes the message length, message type, and the JSON-encoded payload, followed by a second message with its length and type.

```text
'96:0{"sid":"hLOEJXN07AE0GQCNAAAB","upgrades":["websocket"],"pingInterval":25000,"pingTimeout":5000}2:40'  
  
with:  
  
  "96"  # the length of the first message  
  ":"   # a separator between length and content  
  "0"   # the "open" message type  
  '{"sid":"hLOEJXN07AE0GQCNAAAB","upgrades":["websocket"],"pingInterval":25000,"pingTimeout":5000}' # the JSON-encoded handshake data  
  "2"   # the length of the second message  
  ":"   # a separator between length and content  
  "4"   # the "message" message type  
  "0"   # the "open" message type in Socket.IO protocol  
  
```

### Configure Socket.IO Server with Redis Adapter (CommonJS)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/server-options

Demonstrates how to configure the Socket.IO server to use the Redis adapter for inter-process communication. It requires the 'socket.io' and '@socket.io/redis-adapter' packages, along with 'redis' for client connections. The code initializes Redis clients and passes them to the createAdapter function.

```javascript
const { Server } = require("socket.io");
const { createAdapter } = require("@socket.io/redis-adapter");
const { createClient } = require("redis");

const pubClient = createClient({ host: "localhost", port: 6379 });
const subClient = pubClient.duplicate();

const io = new Server({
  adapter: createAdapter(pubClient, subClient)
});

io.listen(3000);
```

### Get Current Transport Name (Server-Side)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/troubleshooting-connection-issues

This server-side JavaScript code snippet retrieves the name of the current transport for a given socket connection. It also provides an event listener to capture the transport name after an upgrade to WebSocket.

```javascript
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  const transport = socket.conn.transport.name; // in most cases, "polling"

  socket.conn.on("upgrade", () => {
    const upgradedTransport = socket.conn.transport.name; // in most cases, "websocket"
  });
});
```

### Client Connection Initialization (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v2/internals

Initiates a Socket.IO client connection to a specified host. This is the first step in establishing a bidirectional communication channel. It relies on the socket.io-client library.

```javascript
const client = io('https://myhost.com');  
```

### Disable v2 Client Compatibility on Socket.IO Server

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Finalizes the upgrade process by disabling support for v2 clients. Setting `allowEIO3` to `false` (the default behavior) will cause v2 clients to receive an 'Unsupported protocol version' error upon connection.

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")({  
  allowEIO3: false  
});  
```

### Managing Client Rooms for Targeted Broadcasting (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/tutorial/api-overview

This snippet illustrates how to use Socket.IO rooms for broadcasting messages to specific subsets of clients. It covers joining a room (`socket.join()`), broadcasting to a room (`io.to().emit()`), broadcasting to clients not in a room (`io.except().emit()`), and leaving a room (`socket.leave()`). Requires a Socket.IO server instance and client connections.

```javascript
io.on('connection', (socket) => {
  // join the room named 'some room'
  socket.join('some room');
    
  // broadcast to all connected clients in the room
  io.to('some room').emit('hello', 'world');
  
  // broadcast to all connected clients except those in the room
  io.except('some room').emit('hello', 'world');
  
  // leave the room
  socket.leave('some room');
});
```

--------------------------------

### Get All Socket IDs in Namespace with Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/server-api

Retrieves a promise that resolves to a Set of socket IDs connected to a specific Socket.IO namespace. This can include filtering by room. If the Redis adapter is used, it can fetch IDs across multiple nodes.

```javascript
// all sockets in the main namespace
const ids = await io.allSockets();

// all sockets in the main namespace and in the "user:1234" room
const ids = await io.in("user:1234").allSockets();

// all sockets in the "chat" namespace
const ids = await io.of("/chat").allSockets();

// all sockets in the "chat" namespace and in the "general" room
const ids = await io.of("/chat").in("general").allSockets();
```

### Manually Triggering Garbage Collection in Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/memory-usage

This example demonstrates how to manually trigger garbage collection in a Socket.IO application. It listens for the 'disconnect' event and calls `gc()` when the last client disconnects. Note that this is primarily for demonstration and not typically needed in production environments as garbage collection is usually automatic.

```javascript
io.on("connection", (socket) => {  
  socket.on("disconnect", () => {  
    const lastToDisconnect = io.of("/").sockets.size === 0;  
    if (lastToDisconnect) {  
      gc();  
    }  
  });  
});  

```

This snippet shows how to include the Socket.IO client library using a script tag. This is one of the ways to make the `io` object available in your client-side JavaScript.

```html
<script src="/socket.io/socket.io.js"></script>
```

--------------------------------

### Manager vs. Socket Query Options (Socket.IO)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Demonstrates the distinction between query parameters for the initial HTTP request (Manager) and the CONNECT packet (Socket). In Socket.IO v3+, the 'query' option for the Socket instance has been renamed to 'auth' to avoid ambiguity. The Manager's 'query' option remains for HTTP request parameters.

```javascript
// Before (ambiguous query)
const socket = io({
  query: {
    token: "abc"
  }
});

// Under the hood (before)
const { Manager } = require("socket.io-client");
const manager = new Manager({
  query: { // sent in the query parameters
    token: "abc"
  }
});
const socket = manager.socket("/", {
  query: { // sent in the CONNECT packet
    token: "abc"
  }
});

// After (clear distinction with 'auth')
// plain object
const socket = io({
  auth: {
    token: "abc"
  }
});

// or with a function
const socket = io({
  auth: (cb) => {
    cb({
      token: "abc"
    });
  }
});

// server-side
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log(socket.handshake.auth.token); // abc
});

```

--------------------------------

### Fetch Socket Instances with Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/server-instance

Retrieves Socket instances based on namespace and room filters. The returned sockets expose properties like id, handshake, rooms, and data, and allow emitting, joining, leaving, and disconnecting.

```javascript
const sockets = await io.fetchSockets();
const sockets = await io.in("room1").fetchSockets();
const sockets = await io.of("/admin").in("room1").fetchSockets();
const sockets = await io.in(theSocketId).fetchSockets();
```

### Configure Cookie Options for Socket.IO Server (v2 vs v3)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Illustrates the modification in how cookie options are configured for Socket.IO servers between v2 and v3. Previously, options like `cookieName`, `cookieHttpOnly`, and `cookiePath` were direct properties. In v3, these are nested under a `cookie` object.

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {
  cookieName: "io",
  cookieHttpOnly: false,
  cookiePath: "/custom"
});
```

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {
  cookie: {
    name: "test",
    httpOnly: false,
    path: "/custom"
  }
});
```

### Socket.IO Polling - Initial Handshake

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/socket-io-protocol

The initial GET request to establish a polling connection with Socket.IO. It includes query parameters for protocol version, transport type, and a cache-busting timestamp. The server responds with session details including a session ID, upgrade options, and ping/pong intervals.

```APIDOC
## GET /socket.io/

### Description
Initiates a polling connection with the Socket.IO server. This request includes protocol version, transport type, and a timestamp for cache busting. The server responds with a session ID and connection parameters.

### Method
GET

### Endpoint
/socket.io/

### Query Parameters
- **EIO** (string) - Required - The Engine.IO protocol version (e.g., "4").
- **transport** (string) - Required - The transport type, "polling" for this example.
- **t** (string) - Required - A timestamp to prevent browser caching.

### Response
#### Success Response (200)
- **sid** (string) - The unique session ID.
- **upgrades** (array) - An array of available upgrade transports (e.g., ["websocket"]).
- **pingInterval** (number) - The interval in milliseconds to send ping packets.
- **pingTimeout** (number) - The timeout in milliseconds to wait for a pong response.
- **maxPayload** (number) - The maximum payload size in bytes.

#### Response Example
```json
{
  "sid": "lv_VI97HAXpY6yYWAAAC",
  "upgrades": [
    "websocket"
  ],
  "pingInterval": 25000,
  "pingTimeout": 5000,
  "maxPayload": 1000000
}
```
```

--------------------------------

### Configure CORS for Socket.IO Server (v2 vs v3)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Demonstrates the change in CORS configuration from Socket.IO v2 to v3. In v2, CORS was handled via `origins` and `handlePreflightRequest` options. In v3, these are replaced by a consolidated `cors` object, which is forwarded to the `cors` package.

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {
  origins: ["https://example.com"],
  // optional, useful for custom headers
  handlePreflightRequest: (req, res) => {
    res.writeHead(200, {
      "Access-Control-Allow-Origin": "https://example.com",
      "Access-Control-Allow-Methods": "GET,POST",
      "Access-Control-Allow-Headers": "my-custom-header",
      "Access-Control-Allow-Credentials": true
    });
    res.end();
  }
});
```

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {
  cors: {
    origin: "https://example.com",
    methods: ["GET", "POST"],
    allowedHeaders: ["my-custom-header"],
    credentials: true
  }
});
```

  // ...

### Handle Asynchronous Errors in Socket.IO Listeners (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/listening-to-events

Provides an example of implementing try-catch blocks within asynchronous event listeners to gracefully handle potential errors during operations like database queries. It ensures that errors are reported back to the client via acknowledgements.

```javascript
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.on("list items", async (callback) => {
    try {
      const items = await findItems();
      callback({
        status: "OK",
        items
      });
    } catch (e) {
      callback({
        status: "NOK"
      });
    }
  });
});
```

### Configure Socket.IO Client for WebSocket Transport Only

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v2/client-api

This example demonstrates how to configure a Socket.IO client to use only the WebSocket transport, skipping the default long-polling connection. It also includes logic to reset transport options upon reconnection attempts if the WebSocket connection fails.

```javascript
const socket = io({
  transports: ['websocket']
});

// on reconnection, reset the transports option, as the Websocket
// connection may have failed (caused by proxy, firewall, browser, ...)
socket.on('reconnect_attempt', () => {
  socket.io.opts.transports = ['polling', 'websocket'];
});
```

--------------------------------

### Accessing Manager Events in Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Explains that Socket instances no longer forward events from their underlying Manager. To access events like connection status, reconnection attempts, or errors, you should now use the 'io' property of the socket to access the Manager instance.

```javascript
socket.on("reconnect_attempt", () => {});
```

```javascript
socket.io.on("reconnect_attempt", () => {});
```

```javascript
io.on('connection', socket => {
  socket.join('some room');

### WebSocket Connection and Query Parameters

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/engine-io-protocol

Details on establishing a WebSocket connection with Socket.IO, including mandatory and optional query parameters required for the transport.

```APIDOC
## WebSocket Transport

The WebSocket transport provides a bidirectional, low-latency communication channel.

### Query Parameters

*   **`EIO`** (string) - Mandatory - The version of the protocol (e.g., `4`).
*   **`transport`** (string) - Mandatory - The name of the transport, must be `websocket`.
*   **`sid`** (string) - Optional - The session ID, used if upgrading from HTTP long-polling.

Failure to provide mandatory parameters will result in the server closing the WebSocket connection.

### Packet Transmission

Each packet (read or write) is transmitted within its own WebSocket frame.

### Connection Limit

A client must not open more than one WebSocket connection per session. Exceeding this limit will cause the server to close the connection.
```

### Enabling `withCredentials` in Socket.IO Client

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

This client-side code snippet shows how to establish a Socket.IO connection with the `withCredentials` option set to `true`. This is crucial for cross-origin requests when the server requires cookies or authorization headers to be sent, ensuring successful communication between the client and a Socket.IO backend configured for CORS with credentials.

```javascript
import { io } from "socket.io-client";

const socket = io("https://backend.domain.com", {
  withCredentials: true
});
```

### Node.js-Specific Options

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/client-initialization

Options for configuring secure connections when using Socket.IO client in a Node.js environment, including certificate and TLS settings.

```APIDOC
## Node.js-Specific Options

*   **Description**: These options are specific to the Node.js environment and are used for configuring TLS and secure connections.

*   **Supported Options**: `agent`, `pfx`, `key`, `passphrase`, `cert`, `ca`, `ciphers`, `rejectUnauthorized`

*   **References**: 
    *   `tls.connect(options[, callback])`
    *   `tls.createSecureContext([options])`

### Example with a self-signed certificate

**Client:**
```javascript
const fs = require("fs");
const socket = require("socket.io-client")("https://example.com", {
  ca: fs.readFileSync("./cert.pem")
});
```

**Server:**
```javascript
const fs = require("fs");
const server = require("https").createServer({
  cert: fs.readFileSync("./cert.pem"),
  key: fs.readFileSync("./key.pem")
});
const io = require("socket.io")(server);
```

### Example with client-certificate authentication

**Client:**
```javascript
const fs = require("fs");
const socket = require("socket.io-client")("https://example.com", {
  ca: fs.readFileSync("./server-cert.pem"),
  cert: fs.readFileSync("./client-cert.pem"),
  key: fs.readFileSync("./client-key.pem")
});
```

**Server:**
```javascript
const fs = require("fs");
const server = require("https").createServer({
  cert: fs.readFileSync("./server-cert.pem"),
  key: fs.readFileSync("./server-key.pem"),
  requestCert: true,
  ca: [
    fs.readFileSync('client-cert.pem')
  ]
});
const io = require("socket.io")(server);
```

**Important Note**: `rejectUnauthorized` is a Node.js-only option and will not bypass security checks in the browser.
```

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/client-options

```javascript
import { io } from "socket.io-client";  
  
const socket = io({  
  transports: ["websocket"],  
  extraHeaders: {  
    "my-custom-header": "1234" // ignored

const io = require('socket.io')();
io.of('/chat').clients((error, clients) => {

});

io.of('/chat').in('general').clients((error, clients) => {

});

io.clients((error, clients) => {

### Set Up SQLite Database and Socket.IO Server (CommonJS)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/tutorial/step-7

Initializes an Express server, sets up a SQLite database to store chat messages with unique offsets, and configures Socket.IO for real-time communication. This code uses CommonJS module syntax.

```javascript
const express = require('express');  
const { createServer } = require('node:http');  
const { join } = require('node:path');  
const { Server } = require('socket.io');  
const sqlite3 = require('sqlite3');  
const { open } = require('sqlite');  
  
async function main() {  
  // open the database file  
  const db = await open({  
    filename: 'chat.db',  
    driver: sqlite3.Database  
  });  
  
  // create our 'messages' table (you can ignore the 'client_offset' column for now)  
  await db.exec(`  
    CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS messages (  
        id INTEGER PRIMARY KEY AUTOINCREMENT,  
        client_offset TEXT UNIQUE,  
        content TEXT  
    );  
  `);  
  
  const app = express();  
  const server = createServer(app);  
  const io = new Server(server, {  
    connectionStateRecovery: {}  
  });  
  
  app.get('/', (req, res) => {  
    res.sendFile(join(__dirname, 'index.html'));  
  });  
  
  io.on('connection', (socket) => {  
    socket.on('chat message', async (msg) => {  
      let result;  
      try {  
        // store the message in the database  
        result = await db.run('INSERT INTO messages (content) VALUES (?)', msg);  
      } catch (e) {  
        // TODO handle the failure  
        return;  
      }  
      // include the offset with the message  
      io.emit('chat message', msg, result.lastID);  
    });  
  });  
  
  server.listen(3000, () => {  
    console.log('server running at http://localhost:3000');  
  });  
}  
  
main();  

```

--------------------------------

### Disable Automatic Reconnection (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/client-initialization

Manually control the reconnection behavior of the Socket.IO client by disabling automatic reconnection. When `reconnection` is set to `false`, manual reconnection logic must be implemented, for example, by listening to the 'close' event and attempting to reopen the connection.

```javascript
import { io } from "socket.io-client";

const socket = io({
  reconnection: false
});

const tryReconnect = () => {
  setTimeout(() => {
    socket.io.open((err) => {
      if (err) {
        tryReconnect();
      }
    });
  }, 2000);
}

socket.io.on("close", tryReconnect);
```

### Get Current Transport Name (Client-Side)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/troubleshooting-connection-issues

This client-side JavaScript code snippet retrieves the name of the current transport being used by the Socket.IO connection. It also includes an event listener to capture the transport name after an upgrade (e.g., from polling to WebSocket).

```javascript
socket.on("connect", () => {
  const transport = socket.io.engine.transport.name; // in most cases, "polling"

  socket.io.engine.on("upgrade", () => {
    const upgradedTransport = socket.io.engine.transport.name; // in most cases, "websocket"
  });
});
```

### Adjust Ping Timeout for Large Payloads

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/troubleshooting-connection-issues

When sending large payloads, the upload time might exceed the `pingTimeout` value, causing disconnections because the heartbeat mechanism fails. This example shows how to increase the `pingTimeout` to 60000 milliseconds (60 seconds).

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {
  pingTimeout: 60000
});
```

--------------------------------

### Socket.IO Join and Leave Operations - Synchronous Execution

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Socket.IO's socket.join() and socket.leave() methods are now synchronous operations. Previously, they were asynchronous to support older Redis adapters. This change simplifies their usage, though custom adapters might still return Promises.

```javascript
socket.join("room1", () => {
 io.to("room1").emit("hello");
});

socket.leave("room2", () => {
  io.to("room2").emit("bye");
});

```

```javascript
socket.join("room1");
io.to("room1").emit("hello");

socket.leave("room2");
io.to("room2").emit("bye");

```

```javascript
// Note: custom adapters may return a Promise, so the previous example becomes:
await socket.join("room1");
io.to("room1").emit("hello");

```

--------------------------------

### Check Socket.IO Reconnection Status After Disconnect (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/client-socket-instance

This code example shows how to check the 'socket.active' attribute within the 'disconnect' event handler. This helps differentiate between temporary disconnections where the socket will attempt to reconnect automatically, and forceful disconnections requiring manual reconnection.

```javascript
socket.on("disconnect", (reason) => {
  if (socket.active) {
    // temporary disconnection, the socket will automatically try to reconnect
  } else {
    // the connection was forcefully closed by the server or the client itself
    // in that case, `socket.connect()` must be manually called in order to reconnect
    console.log(reason);
  }
});
```

--------------------------------

### Socket.IO with Redis Adapter (redis package, cluster)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/redis-adapter

Sets up a Socket.IO server with the Redis adapter, connecting to a Redis cluster using the 'redis' package. This configuration is suitable for highly available Socket.IO deployments.

```javascript
import { createCluster } from "redis";  
import { Server } from "socket.io";  
import { createAdapter } from "@socket.io/redis-adapter";  
  
const pubClient = createCluster({  
  rootNodes: [  
    {  
      url: "redis://localhost:7000",  
    },  
    {  
      url: "redis://localhost:7001",  
    },  
    {  
      url: "redis://localhost:7002",  
    },  
  ],  
});  
const subClient = pubClient.duplicate();  
  
await Promise.all([  
  pubClient.connect(),  
  subClient.connect()  
]);  
  
const io = new Server({  
  adapter: createAdapter(pubClient, subClient)  
});  
  
io.listen(3000);  


```

--------------------------------

### Socket.IO Namespace Connected Sockets Access - connected to sockets Map

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

The Namespace.connected object in Socket.IO, which stored connected sockets, has been renamed to Namespace.sockets and is now an ES6 Map. This change affects how to retrieve a socket by its ID or iterate through all connected sockets within a namespace.

```javascript
// get a socket by ID in the main namespace
const socket = io.of("/").connected[socketId];

// get a socket by ID in the "admin" namespace
const socket = io.of("/admin").connected[socketId];

// loop through all sockets
const sockets = io.of("/").connected;
for (const id in sockets) {
  if (sockets.hasOwnProperty(id)) {
    const socket = sockets[id];
    // ...
  }
}

// get the number of connected sockets
const count = Object.keys(io.of("/").connected).length;

```

```javascript
// get a socket by ID in the main namespace
const socket = io.of("/").sockets.get(socketId);

// get a socket by ID in the "admin" namespace
const socket = io.of("/admin").sockets.get(socketId);

// loop through all sockets
for (const [_, socket] of io.of("/").sockets) {
  // ...
}

// get the number of connected sockets
const count = io.of("/").sockets.size;

```

--------------------------------

### Emit Local Event with Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/server-api

Emits an event with the 'local' flag, ensuring that the event data is broadcast only to the current node. This is particularly useful when using a distributed setup, like with the Redis adapter, to prevent unnecessary broadcasting across all connected servers.

```javascript
io.local.emit("an event", { some: "data" });
```

### Socket.IO with Redis Adapter (ioredis package)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/redis-adapter

Configures a Socket.IO server to use the Redis adapter with the 'ioredis' package. This is an alternative to the 'redis' package and is often recommended due to better subscription handling.

```javascript
import { Redis } from "ioredis";  
import { Server } from "socket.io";  
import { createAdapter } from "@socket.io/redis-adapter";  
  
const pubClient = new Redis();  
const subClient = pubClient.duplicate();  
  
const io = new Server({  
  adapter: createAdapter(pubClient, subClient)  
});  
  
io.listen(3000);  


```

### Socket.IO Server with HTTP/2 (CommonJS)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/server-initialization

Sets up a Socket.IO server using Node.js HTTP/2 module and CommonJS. This enables multiplexing and header compression. Requires SSL certificate and key files. Listens for connections on port 3000.

```javascript
const { readFileSync } = require("fs");  
const { createSecureServer } = require("http2");  
const { Server } = require("socket.io");  
  
const httpServer = createSecureServer({
  allowHTTP1: true,  
  key: readFileSync("/path/to/my/key.pem"),  
  cert: readFileSync("/path/to/my/cert.pem")  
});  
  
const io = new Server(httpServer, { /* options */ });  
  
io.on("connection", (socket) => {  
  // ...  
});  
  
httpServer.listen(3000);  


```

### Socket.IO io.to() Immutability Change (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-3-x-to-4-0

Demonstrates the change in Socket.IO's io.to() method from mutating to immutable. Previously, broadcasting to a room would mutate the io instance. Now, it returns an immutable instance, requiring a new approach for chained broadcasts or specific room targeting.

```javascript
const operator1 = io.to("room1");
const operator2 = operator1.to("room2");
const operator3 = socket.broadcast;
const operator4 = socket.to("room3").to("room4");

operator1.emit(/* ... */); // only to clients in "room1"
operator2.emit(/* ... */); // to clients in "room1" or in "room2"
operator3.emit(/* ... */); // to all clients but the sender
operator4.emit(/* ... */); // to clients in "room3" or in "room4" but the sender
```

### Registering a Basic Socket.IO Middleware

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/middlewares

Demonstrates how to register a middleware function in Socket.IO. The middleware receives the socket instance and a `next` callback. It checks for a valid request and either proceeds to the next middleware or returns an error. This is crucial for request validation before full connection establishment.

```javascript
io.use((socket, next) => {
  if (isValid(socket.request)) {
    next();
  } else {
    next(new Error("invalid"));
  }
});
```

### Configure CORS for Localhost Development with Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/handling-cors

This snippet shows how to configure Socket.IO to allow connections from a specific localhost port during development. It's crucial for testing applications where the frontend and backend might be served from different ports. The example explicitly sets the origin to 'http://localhost:8080'.

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {  
  cors: {  
    origin: "http://localhost:8080",  
    methods: ["GET", "POST"]
  }
});  

httpServer.listen(3000);
```

### Event Emission from Socket to Manager (Socket.IO)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Explains the change in event forwarding for Socket.IO. Previously, Socket instances emitted events related to the underlying connection. Now, these events are only available on the Manager instance (accessible via `socket.io`). This clarifies event sources and prevents potential confusion.

```javascript
// Before
socket.on("reconnect_attempt", () => {});

// After
socket.io.on("reconnect_attempt", () => {});

```

### Enable Socket.IO v2 Client Compatibility (allowEIO3)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/server-initialization

Enables compatibility with Socket.IO v2 clients. Defaults to false. Useful when migrating from older versions.

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {  
  allowEIO3: true // false by default  
});  
```

### Configure Cookies for Cross-Origin Communication in Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

When your frontend is served from a different domain than your backend, you need to explicitly enable cookies for cross-origin requests. On the server, set `credentials: true` within the `cors` configuration. On the client, use the `withCredentials: true` option when establishing the connection.

```javascript
import { Server } from "socket.io";

const io = new Server({
  cors: {
    origin: ["https://front.domain.com"],
    credentials: true
  }
});

```

```javascript
import { io } from "socket.io-client";

const socket = io("https://backend.domain.com", {
  withCredentials: true
});

```

socket.on('hello', (arg) => {
  console.log(arg); // 'world'
});

### Joining User ID as a Room with Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/rooms

A sample use case demonstrating how to join a user to a room identified by their user ID to broadcast data specific to that user across their devices.

```javascript
function computeUserIdFromHeaders(headers) {  
  // to be implemented  
}  

io.on("connection", async (socket) => {  
  const userId = await computeUserIdFromHeaders(socket.handshake.headers);  
  
  socket.join(userId);  
  
  // and then later  
  io.to(userId).emit("hi");  
});  
```

### Enable CORS for Socket.IO v3 and Later

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Socket.IO versions 3 and later require explicit enabling of Cross-Origin Resource Sharing (CORS). If you encounter 'Cross-Origin Request Blocked' errors, you must configure the CORS settings. Refer to the official Socket.IO documentation for detailed guidance on handling CORS.

```text
Cross-Origin Request Blocked: The Same Origin Policy disallows reading the remote resource at xxx/socket.io/?EIO=4&transport=polling&t=NMnp2WI. (Reason: CORS header ‘Access-Control-Allow-Origin’ missing).
```

### Clear Internal Buffer on Reconnect (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/client-offline-behavior

This example demonstrates how to clear the internal event buffer of a Socket.IO socket upon reconnection. By setting 'socket.sendBuffer' to an empty array within the 'connect' event handler, you ensure that no buffered events are sent after the connection is restored.

```javascript
socket.on("connect", () => {
  socket.sendBuffer = [];
});
```

### Socket.IO Client Initialization: Reusing Manager Instances

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/client-initialization

Demonstrates how Socket.IO manages connections using Manager instances. By default, multiple Socket instances connecting to the same origin and namespace will reuse the same Manager, optimizing connection resources. The `forceNew: true` option explicitly creates a new Manager, leading to separate connections.

```javascript
const socket = io("https://example.com"); // the main namespace  
const productSocket = io("https://example.com/product"); // the "product" namespace  
const orderSocket = io("https://example.com/order"); // the "order" namespace  

const socket = io("https://example.com"); // the main namespace  
const productSocket = io("https://example.com/product", { forceNew: true }); // the "product" namespace  
const orderSocket = io("https://example.com/order", { forceNew: true }); // the "order" namespace  

const socket1 = io(); // 1st manager  
const socket2 = io(); // 2nd manager  
const socket3 = io("/admin"); // reuse the 1st manager  
const socket4 = io("/admin"); // 3rd manager  
```

### Managing Rooms Within Namespaces (Node.js)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/namespaces

Illustrates how to manage rooms within different Socket.IO namespaces. It shows joining a room ('room1') in both the '/orders' and '/users' namespaces and emitting messages to that room. Importantly, it highlights that rooms are namespace-specific.

```javascript
const orderNamespace = io.of("/orders");

orderNamespace.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.join("room1");
  orderNamespace.to("room1").emit("hello");
});

const userNamespace = io.of("/users");

userNamespace.on("connection", (socket) => {
  socket.join("room1"); // distinct from the room in the "orders" namespace
  userNamespace.to("room1").emit("holà");
});
```

### Socket.IO Event-Based Packet Decoding

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v2/migrating-from-0-9

Uses an event-based decoder for handling packets that may be encoded in multiple parts, such as those containing binary data. The `Decoder` class emits a 'decoded' event when a complete packet is received. It requires adding encoded segments incrementally using `decoder.add()`.

```javascript
var decoder = new parser.Decoder();
decoder.on('decoded', function(packet) {
  console.log(packet); // formed socket.io packet to handle
});
decoder.add(encodings[0]); // say encodings is array of two encodings received from transport
decoder.add(encodings[1]); // after adding the last element, 'decoded' is emitted from decoder
```

### Main Namespace Equivalents (JavaScript)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/namespaces

Explains that direct calls on the `io` instance for connection events, middleware, and broadcasting are equivalent to using `io.of("/")`. This clarifies the behavior of the default namespace.

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io");

// Using the io instance directly (main namespace)
io.on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log("Client connected to main namespace (via io)");
});

io.use((socket, next) => {
  console.log("Middleware for main namespace (via io)");
  next();
});

io.emit("hello", "Broadcast from main namespace (via io)");

// Equivalent using io.of("/")
io.of("/").on("connection", (socket) => {
  console.log("Client connected to main namespace (via io.of('/'))");
});

io.of("/").use((socket, next) => {
  console.log("Middleware for main namespace (via io.of('/'))");
  next();
});

io.of("/").emit("hello", "Broadcast from main namespace (via io.of('/'))");
```

--------------------------------

### Middleware Error Handling (Socket.IO)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

Illustrates the change in how middleware errors are handled and emitted in Socket.IO. Previously, a generic 'error' event was used, and the emitted object could be an Error or a plain object. Now, the 'connect_error' event is used, and it always emits an actual Error object with an optional 'data' property.

```javascript
// server-side (before)
io.use((socket, next) => {
  next(new Error("not authorized"));
});

// client-side (before)
socket.on("error", err => {
  console.log(err); // not authorized
});

// or with an object
// server-side (before)
io.use((socket, next) => {
  const err = new Error("not authorized");
  err.data = { content: "Please retry later" }; // additional details
  next(err);
});

// client-side (before)
socket.on("error", err => {
  console.log(err); // { content: "Please retry later" }
});

// server-side (after)
io.use((socket, next) => {
  const err = new Error("not authorized");
  err.data = { content: "Please retry later" }; // additional details
  next(err);
});

// client-side (after)
socket.on("connect_error", err => {
  console.log(err instanceof Error); // true
  console.log(err.message); // not authorized
  console.log(err.data); // { content: "Please retry later" }
});

```

--------------------------------

### Emit Event to All Except Sender (Server-Side)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/tutorial/step-5

This code shows how to send an event to all connected sockets except for the one that initiated the event, using `socket.broadcast.emit()`. This is common for scenarios like user presence notifications or chat messages where the sender doesn't need to see their own message echoed back immediately. It requires a Socket.IO server with an active connection.

```javascript
io.on('connection', (socket) => {
  socket.broadcast.emit('hi');
});
```

### Engine.IO Protocol Overview

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/engine-io-protocol

Provides a high-level overview of the Engine.IO protocol and its relationship with WebSocket and Socket.IO.

```APIDOC
## Engine.IO Protocol Overview

### Description
Engine.IO is a protocol that enables full-duplex and low-overhead communication between a client and a server. It is built upon the WebSocket protocol and uses HTTP long-polling as a fallback mechanism if a WebSocket connection cannot be established.

### Relationship with Socket.IO
The Socket.IO protocol is built on top of the Engine.IO protocol, extending its capabilities by adding features over the communication channel provided by Engine.IO.
```

### Broadcasting to a Room with Socket.IO

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/rooms

Shows how to emit an event to all sockets within a specified room. The `to` and `in` methods are interchangeable.

```javascript
io.to("some room").emit("some event");  
```

### Socket.IO Namespace Connection - Default Namespace Behavior Change

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

In Socket.IO, clients no longer implicitly connect to the default namespace ('/'). Middleware and event handlers registered for the default namespace are not triggered when a client explicitly connects to another namespace. The term 'main' namespace is now used instead of 'default'.

```javascript
// client-side
const socket = io("/admin");

// server-side
io.use((socket, next) => {
  // not triggered anymore
});

io.on("connection", socket => {
  // not triggered anymore
})

io.of("/admin").use((socket, next) => {
  // triggered
});

```

--------------------------------

### Standalone Socket.IO Server with Postgres Adapter

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/postgres-adapter

Sets up a Socket.IO server using the Postgres adapter. It initializes a PostgreSQL connection pool, creates the necessary attachment table if it doesn't exist, and configures the adapter for inter-server communication.

```typescript
import { Server } from "socket.io";  
import { createAdapter } from "@socket.io/postgres-adapter";  
import pg from "pg";  
  
const io = new Server();  
  
const pool = new pg.Pool({  
  user: "postgres",  
  host: "localhost",  
  database: "postgres",  
  password: "changeit",  
  port: 5432,  
});  
  
pool.query(`  
  CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS socket_io_attachments (  
      id          bigserial UNIQUE,  
      created_at  timestamptz DEFAULT NOW(),  
      payload     bytea  
  );
`);  
  
pool.on("error", (err) => {  
  console.error("Postgres error", err);  
});  
  
io.adapter(createAdapter(pool));  
io.listen(3000);  
```

### Remove @types/socket.io-client for TypeScript Projects

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v3/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

In TypeScript projects using Socket.IO, the `@types/socket.io-client` package can conflict with the built-in typings from `socket.io-client`. This conflict may lead to errors like 'Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'extraHeaders' does not exist'. Remove the separate types package to resolve this.

```typescript
Object literal may only specify known properties, and 'extraHeaders' does not exist in type 'ConnectOpts'
```

### Socket.IO Server Options Overview

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/server-options

A comprehensive list of available options for configuring a Socket.IO server, categorized into general server options and low-level engine options.

```APIDOC
## Socket.IO Server Options

### Description
This outlines the various configuration options available when creating a Socket.IO server instance.

### Method
GET

### Endpoint
/options

### Parameters
#### Query Parameters
(No query parameters for this overview)

#### Request Body
(Not applicable for overview)

### Response
#### Success Response (200)
- **serverOptions** (object) - Contains a list of general Socket.IO server options.
  - **adapter** (object) - The adapter to use for scaling Socket.IO between multiple Nodes.
  - **cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces** (boolean) - Whether to remove empty child namespaces automatically. Defaults to `false`.
  - **connectionStateRecovery** (object) - Configuration for connection state recovery.
  - **connectTimeout** (number) - Timeout for establishing a connection in ms. Defaults to `45000`.
  - **parser** (object) - The parser to use for encoding and decoding messages. Defaults to `socket.io-parser`.
  - **path** (string) - The path that the Socket.IO server should listen to. Defaults to `/socket.io`.
  - **serveClient** (boolean) - Whether to serve the static client files. Defaults to `true`.
- **engineOptions** (object) - Options for the underlying WebSocket engine.
  - **addTrailingSlash** (boolean) - Whether to add a trailing slash to the request URL. Defaults to `false`.
  - **allowEIO3** (boolean) - Whether to allow connections from Socket.IO v3 clients. Defaults to `true`.
  - **allowRequest** (function) - A function that allows or denies requests. Defaults to `null`.
  - **allowUpgrades** (boolean) - Whether to allow transport upgrades. Defaults to `true`.
  - **cookie** (object | string) - Options for the cookie used for session identification.
  - **cors** (object) - CORS configuration.
  - **httpCompression** (object) - Options for HTTP compression.
  - **maxHttpBufferSize** (string) - Maximum buffer size for HTTP transports. Defaults to `10E6`.
  - **perMessageDeflate** (object) - Options for per-message deflate compression.
  - **pingInterval** (number) - How often the server should send a ping packet.
  - **pingTimeout** (number) - How many ms without a pong packet the server should disconnect the client.
  - **transports** (array) - An array of allowed transport methods. Defaults to `["polling", "websocket"]`.
  - **upgradeTimeout** (number) - How many ms the polling should wait for an upgrade to a websocket.
  - **wsEngine** (object) - The WebSocket server implementation to use. Defaults to `require("ws").Server`.

#### Response Example
```json
{
  "serverOptions": {
    "adapter": null,
    "cleanupEmptyChildNamespaces": false,
    "connectionStateRecovery": {},
    "connectTimeout": 45000,
    "parser": {},
    "path": "/socket.io",
    "serveClient": true
  },
  "engineOptions": {
    "addTrailingSlash": false,
    "allowEIO3": true,
    "allowRequest": null,
    "allowUpgrades": true,
    "cookie": {},
    "cors": {},
    "httpCompression": {},
    "maxHttpBufferSize": "10E6",
    "perMessageDeflate": {},
    "pingInterval": 25000,
    "pingTimeout": 20000,
    "transports": ["polling", "websocket"],
    "upgradeTimeout": 10000,
    "wsEngine": { /* ws.Server */ }
  }
}
```
```

### Adjusting maxHttpBufferSize in Socket.IO Server

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v4/migrating-from-2-x-to-3-0

This snippet demonstrates how to increase the `maxHttpBufferSize` when initializing the Socket.IO server to handle large payloads. The default value is 1MB, and increasing it prevents clients from being disconnected when sending data exceeding this limit. This is useful for applications dealing with frequent or large data transmissions.

```javascript
const io = require("socket.io")(httpServer, {
  maxHttpBufferSize: 1e8
});
```

## socket.prependAny(callback)​

Register a new catch-all listener. The listener is added to the beginning of the listeners array, meaning it will be executed before other `onAny` listeners.

#### Response Example
None

### Socket Basics

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v2/client-api

Understanding the fundamental `Socket` class in Socket.IO, its properties, and basic usage for emitting and receiving events.

```APIDOC
## Socket Basics

A `Socket` is the fundamental class for interacting with the server. A `Socket` belongs to a certain Namespace (by default `/`) and uses an underlying Manager to communicate. A `Socket` is basically an EventEmitter which sends events to — and receive events from — the server over the network.

### `socket.id`

*   **Description**: A unique identifier for the socket session. Set after the `connect` event is triggered, and updated after the `reconnect` event.
*   **Type**: String

```javascript
const socket = io('http://localhost');

console.log(socket.id); // undefined initially

socket.on('connect', () => {
  console.log(socket.id); // e.g., 'G5p5...'
});
```

### `socket.connected`

*   **Description**: Indicates whether or not the socket is connected to the server.
*   **Type**: Boolean

```javascript
const socket = io('http://localhost');

socket.on('connect', () => {
  console.log(socket.connected); // true
});
```

### `socket.disconnected`

*   **Description**: Indicates whether or not the socket is disconnected from the server.
*   **Type**: Boolean

```javascript
const socket = io('http://localhost');

socket.on('connect', () => {
  console.log(socket.disconnected); // false
});
```

### Emitting and Receiving Events

```javascript
// Emit an event with data
socket.emit('hello', { a: 'b', c: [] });

// Listen for an event
socket.on('hey', (...args) => {
  // Handle received data
  console.log(args);
});
```
```

--------------------------------

### Connect to Socket.IO with URL Query Parameters (Client & Server)

Source: https://socket.io/docs/v2/client-api

Shows how to pass query parameters directly within the connection URL for authentication or configuration. The server-side code includes middleware to validate these parameters.

```javascript
// client-side  
const socket = io('http://localhost?token=abc');  
  
// server-side  
const io = require('socket.io')();  
  
// middleware  
io.use((socket, next) => {  
  let token = socket.handshake.query.token;  
  if (isValid(token)) {  
    return next();  
  }  
  return next(new Error('authentication error'));  
});  
  
// then  
io.on('connection', (socket) => {  
  let token = socket.handshake.query.token;  
  // ...  
});  

```
